[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Allowed imports: numpy (use it for board analysis--masks, unique, argwhere, diffs), bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

- Each call has a 30-second limit and about 1024 output tokens. Keep output compact; truncation is reported.

ascii = current_frame.ascii
lines = ascii.split('\n')

# Determine walkable cells (B is floor, b is player, N is goal)
walkable = set()
for r in range(64):
    for c in range(64):
        ch = lines[r][c]
        if ch in 'BbN':  # floor, player, goal
            walkable.add((r,c))

# Player position (blue block)
player_cells = [(r,c) for r in range(64) for c in range(64) if lines[r][c] == 'b']
print("Player cells:", player_cells)
print("Player bounding box:", min(r for r,c in player_cells), max(r for r,c in player_cells), min(c for r,c in player_cells), max(c for r,c in player_cells))

# Goal cells
goal_cells = [(r,c) for r in range(64) for c in range(64) if lines[r][c] == 'N']
print("Goal cells:", goal_cells)
print("Goal bounding box:", min(r for r,c in goal_cells), max(r for r,c in goal_cells), min(c for r,c in goal_cells), max(c for r,c in goal_cells))

# Check if player can reach goal via BFS
from collections import deque
start = player_cells[0]
goal_set = set(goal_cells)
q = deque([start])
visited = {start}
while q:
    r,c = q.popleft()
    for dr,dc in [(0,1),(0,-1),(1,0),(-1,0)]:
        nr,nc = r+dr,c+dc
        if (nr,nc) in walkable and (nr,nc) not in visited:
            visited.add((nr,nc))
            q.append((nr,nc))

print("Can reach goal:", any(g in visited for g in goal_set))
print("Visited count:", len(visited))
print("Total walkable:", len(walkable))

Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

# Let me trace the maze corridors. The maze is made of 3x3 wall blocks (g and W).
# The corridors are 3 cells wide. Let me map the walkable B cells and find the path.
# But the player is a 3x3 block, so I need to check that the entire 3x3 area fits.

ascii = current_frame.ascii
lines = ascii.split('\n')

# Let me identify all wall cells (g and W) and floor cells (B)
# The player is 3x3, so for the player to be at position (r,c) (top-left of 3x3),
# all cells (r..r+2, c..c+2) must be walkable (B).

# First, let me understand the maze structure. Let me find all B (floor) regions.
# The maze seems to be built on a grid where walls are 3x3 blocks.
# Let me look at the overall pattern of the maze.

# Let me trace the path from player to goal using BFS for the 3x3 player
from collections import deque

# Player top-left at (15,15), goal top-left at (45,45)
# Player is 3x3, so it occupies rows 15-17, cols 15-17

# Check if a 3x3 block at (r,c) is valid (all cells walkable)
def can_place(r, c):
    if r < 0 or r+2 >= 64 or c < 0 or c+2 >= 64:
        return False
    for dr in range(3):
        for dc in range(3):
            if lines[r+dr][c+dc] not in 'BbN':
                return False
    return True

# BFS for the 3x3 player
start = (15, 15)
goal = (45, 45)
q = deque([(start, [])])
visited = {start}
while q:
    (r, c), path = q.popleft()
    if (r, c) == goal:
        print("Found path:", path)
        print("Path length:", len(path))
        break
    for (dr, dc, dir_name) in [(0,1,'RIGHT'),(0,-1,'LEFT'),(1,0,'DOWN'),(-1,0,'UP')]:
        nr, nc = r+dr, c+dc
        if (nr, nc) not in visited and can_place(nr, nc):
            visited.add((nr, nc))
            q.append(((nr, nc), path + [dir_name]))
else:
    print("No path found")
    print("Visited states:", len(visited))

def can_place(r, c):
    """Check if a 3x3 block at top-left (r,c) is fully walkable."""
    if r < 0 or r+2 >= 64 or c < 0 or c+2 >= 64:
        return False
    for dr in range(3):
        for dc in range(3):
            if lines[r+dr][c+dc] not in 'BbN':

    return True

from collections import deque

start = (0, 0)
goal = (10, 10)

def is_floor(r, c):
    if r < 0 or r >= 11 or c < 0 or c >= 11:
        return False
    return grid[r][c] in '?BN'

q = deque([(start, [])])
visited = {start}
found = False
while q and not found:
    (r, c), path = q.popleft()
    if (r, c) == goal:
        print("Path found, length:", len(path))
        print("Path:", path)
        found = True
        break
    for (dr, dc, dname) in [(0,1,'RIGHT'),(0,-1,'LEFT'),(1,0,'DOWN'),(-1,0,'UP')]:
        nr, nc = r+dr, c+dc
        if (nr, nc) not in visited and is_floor(nr, nc):
            visited.add((nr, nc))
            q.append(((nr, nc), path + [dname]))

if not found:
    print("No path found within maze")
    print("Visited blocks:", len(visited))
    print("Visited positions:", sorted(visited))

def can_place_gw(r, c):
    """Check if 3x3 block at (r,c) fits in walkable g/W cells."""
    if r < 0 or r+2 >= 64 or c < 0 or c+2 >= 64:
        return False

- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Allowed imports: numpy (use it for board analysis--masks, unique, argwhere, diffs), bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

- Each call has a 30-second limit and about 1024 output tokens. Keep output compact; truncation is reported.

# Player at rows 15-17, cols 21-23
# Goal at rows 45-47, cols 45-47

    for c in range(64):
        if lines[r][c] == 'b':
            player_cells.append((r, c))
pr, pc = min(r for r,c in player_cells), min(c for r,c in player_cells)
print(f"Player top-left: ({pr},{pc})")

# Goal position

for r in range(64):
    for c in range(64):
        if lines[r][c] == 'N':
            goal_cells.append((r, c))

print(f"Goal top-left: ({gr},{gc})")

# The player slides in a direction until it hits a wall.
# Let me define the slide function.
def slide(r, c, dr, dc):
    """Slide a 3x3 block from (r,c) in direction (dr,dc) until it hits a wall."""
    nr, nc = r, c
    while True:
        tr, tc = nr + dr, nc + dc
        # Check if the 3x3 block at (tr,tc) fits in walkable cells
        if tr < 0 or tr+2 >= 64 or tc < 0 or tc+2 >= 64:
            break
        ok = True
        for rr in range(3):
            for cc in range(3):
                if lines[tr+rr][tc+cc] not in 'gWbN':
                    ok = False
                    break
            if not ok:
                break
        if not ok:
            break
        nr, nc = tr, tc
    return (nr, nc)

# BFS from player to goal using slide moves
from collections import deque
start = (pr, pc)
goal = (gr, gc)
q = deque([(start, [])])
visited = {start}
found = False
while q and not found:
    (r, c), path = q.popleft()
    if (r, c) == goal:
        print("Path found, length:", len(path))
        print("Path:", path)
        found = True
        break
    for (dr, dc, dname) in [(0,1,'RIGHT'),(0,-1,'LEFT'),(1,0,'DOWN'),(-1,0,'UP')]:
        nr, nc = slide(r, c, dr, dc)
        if (nr, nc) != (r, c) and (nr, nc) not in visited:

# Let me redo the BFS. The player is at (15,21).
# Walkable: g, W, b, c, N
# Wall: B

ascii = current_frame.ascii
lines = ascii.split('\n')

def slide(r, c, dr, dc):
    """Slide a 3x3 block from (r,c) in direction (dr,dc) until it hits a wall."""
    nr, nc = r, c
    while True:
        tr, tc = nr + dr, nc + dc
        if tr < 0 or tr+2 >= 64 or tc < 0 or tc+2 >= 64:
            break
        ok = True
        for rr in range(3):
            for cc in range(3):
                ch = lines[tr+rr][tc+cc]
                if ch not in 'gWbNc':
                    ok = False
                    break
            if not ok:
                break
        if not ok:
            break
        nr, nc = tr, tc
    return (nr, nc)

from collections import deque
start = (15, 21)
goal = (45, 45)
q = deque([(start, [])])
visited = {start}
found = False
while q and not found:
    (r, c), path = q.popleft()
    if (r, c) == goal:
        print("Path found, length:", len(path))
        print("Path:", path)
        found = True
        break
    for (dr, dc, dname) in [(0,1,'RIGHT'),(0,-1,'LEFT'),(1,0,'DOWN'),(-1,0,'UP')]:
        nr, nc = slide(r, c, dr, dc)
        if (nr, nc) != (r, c) and (nr, nc) not in visited:
            visited.add((nr, nc))
            q.append(((nr, nc), path + [dname]))

if not found:
    print("No path found")
    print("Visited:", len(visited))
    print("Visited positions:", sorted(visited))
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Allowed imports: numpy (use it for board analysis--masks, unique, argwhere, diffs), bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Allowed imports: numpy (use it for board analysis--masks, unique, argwhere, diffs), bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

# BFS from player to goal using 2-block (6-cell) moves.
ascii = current_frame.ascii
lines = ascii.split('\n')

def can_move(r, c):
    """Check if a 3x3 block at (r,c) fits in walkable cells."""
    if r < 0 or r+2 >= 64 or c < 0 or c+2 >= 64:
        return False
    for rr in range(3):
        for cc in range(3):
            if lines[r+rr][c+cc] not in 'gWbNc':
                return False
    return True

from collections import deque
start = (27, 21)
goal = (45, 45)
q = deque([(start, [])])
visited = {start}
found = False
while q and not found:
    (r, c), path = q.popleft()
    if (r, c) == goal:
        print("Path found, length:", len(path))
        print("Path:", path)
        found = True
        break
    for (dr, dc, dname) in [(0,1,'RIGHT'),(0,-1,'LEFT'),(1,0,'DOWN'),(-1,0,'UP')]:
        nr, nc = r + dr*6, c + dc*6
        if (nr, nc) not in visited and can_move(nr, nc):
            visited.add((nr, nc))
            q.append(((nr, nc), path + [dname]))

if not found:
    print("No path found")
    print("Visited:", len(visited))
    print("Visited positions:", sorted(visited))
</parameter>

# The player moves 2 blocks (6 cells) per action, but the path must be clear.
# Let me redo the BFS with this mechanic.

ascii = current_frame.ascii
lines = ascii.split('\n')

def can_move(r, c, dr, dc):
    """Check if the player can move from (r,c) by 2 blocks in direction (dr,dc).
    The entire path (all 3x3 cells at each intermediate position) must be walkable."""
    # Check the destination
    nr, nc = r + dr*6, c + dc*6
    if nr < 0 or nr+2 >= 64 or nc < 0 or nc+2 >= 64:
        return False
    # Check all intermediate positions (including destination)
    for step in range(1, 3):
        tr, tc = r + dr*step*3, c + dc*step*3
        for rr in range(3):
            for cc in range(3):
                if lines[tr+rr][tc+cc] not in 'gWbNc':
                    return False
    return True

# Player at (27,27)
pr, pc = 27, 27
for (dr, dc, name) in [(0,1,'RIGHT'),(0,-1,'LEFT'),(1,0,'DOWN'),(-1,0,'UP')]:
    ok = can_move(pr, pc, dr, dc)
    nr, nc = pr + dr*6, pc + dc*6
    print(f"  {name} to ({nr},{nc}): {'OK' if ok else 'BLOCKED'}")

# Now BFS
from collections import deque
start = (pr, pc)
goal = (45, 45)
q = deque([(start, [])])
visited = {start}
found = False
while q and not found:
    (r, c), path = q.popleft()
    if (r, c) == goal:
        print("\nPath found, length:", len(path))
        print("Path:", path)
        found = True
        break
    for (dr, dc, dname) in [(0,1,'RIGHT'),(0,-1,'LEFT'),(1,0,'DOWN'),(-1,0,'UP')]:
        nr, nc = r + dr*6, c + dc*6
        if (nr, nc) not in visited and can_move(r, c, dr, dc):
            visited.add((nr, nc))
            q.append(((nr, nc), path + [dname]))

if not found:
    print("\nNo path found")
    print("Visited:", len(visited))
    print("Visited positions:", sorted(visited))

# Verify the BFS path from (27,27) to (45,45)
# Path: UP, RIGHT, DOWN, DOWN, LEFT, LEFT, DOWN, RIGHT, RIGHT, DOWN, RIGHT, UP, RIGHT, DOWN

ascii = current_frame.ascii
lines = ascii.split('\n')

def can_move(r, c, dr, dc):
    """Check if the player can move from (r,c) by 2 blocks in direction (dr,dc).
    The entire path (all cells between current and destination) must be walkable."""
    nr, nc = r + dr*6, c + dc*6
    if nr < 0 or nr+2 >= 64 or nc < 0 or nc+2 >= 64:
        return False
    # Check all cells in the 3x6 or 6x3 rectangle between current and destination
    for step in range(1, 3):
        tr, tc = r + dr*step*3, c + dc*step*3
        for rr in range(3):
            for cc in range(3):
                if lines[tr+rr][tc+cc] not in 'gWbNc':
                    return False
    return True

path = ['UP', 'RIGHT', 'DOWN', 'DOWN', 'LEFT', 'LEFT', 'DOWN', 'RIGHT', 'RIGHT', 'DOWN', 'RIGHT', 'UP', 'RIGHT', 'DOWN']

        break

print(f"End: {pos}")

def can_move(r, c, dr, dc):
    nr, nc = r + dr*6, c + dc*6
    if nr < 0 or nr+2 >= 64 or nc < 0 or nc+2 >= 64:
        return False
    for step in range(1, 3):
        tr, tc = r + dr*step*3, c + dc*step*3
        for rr in range(3):
            for cc in range(3):
                if lines[tr+rr][tc+cc] not in 'gWbNc':
                    return False
    return True

        all_valid = False
        break

# BFS from player (33,12) to goal (21,48) on level 2
ascii = current_frame.ascii
lines = ascii.split('\n')

def can_move(r, c, dr, dc):
    """Check if the player can move from (r,c) by 2 blocks in direction (dr,dc)."""
    nr, nc = r + dr*6, c + dc*6
    if nr < 0 or nr+2 >= 64 or nc < 0 or nc+2 >= 64:
        return False
    for step in range(1, 3):
        tr, tc = r + dr*step*3, c + dc*step*3
        for rr in range(3):
            for cc in range(3):
                if lines[tr+rr][tc+cc] not in 'gWbNcRp':
                    return False
    return True

from collections import deque
start = (33, 12)
goal = (21, 48)
q = deque([(start, [])])
visited = {start}
found = False
while q and not found:
    (r, c), path = q.popleft()
    if (r, c) == goal:
        print("Path found, length:", len(path))
        print("Path:", path)
        found = True
        break
    for (dr, dc, dname) in [(0,1,'RIGHT'),(0,-1,'LEFT'),(1,0,'DOWN'),(-1,0,'UP')]:
        nr, nc = r + dr*6, c + dc*6
        if (nr, nc) not in visited and can_move(r, c, dr, dc):
            visited.add((nr, nc))
            q.append(((nr, nc), path + [dname]))

if not found:
    print("No path found")
    print("Visited:", len(visited))
    print("Visited positions:", sorted(visited))

Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. - Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. lines = ascii.split('\n')

for r in range(21, 37):

            row += '.'
        elif ch in 'bc':
